I’ll fix my hair because I think maybe if it falls a certain way it hides the uneven part, but then I mess with it too much and it looks stupid, so I start over. Sometimes I change shirts because the collar or neckline makes my jaw look more crooked, or it makes me feel like people will look right there.\n\nAnd I’ll ask my mom, like, “Does it look weird?” or “Can you tell?” but even if she says no, I’ll want to ask again because it only helps for maybe a minute. So it’s not a certain number, but it becomes this whole routine before school, and I can get stuck in it for so long that I miss lab, or if I do go, I’m already drained and can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I worry about school and getting behind, but that’s usually because of this. Like, if I miss lab or show up late, then I start worrying about what that means, or whether people think I’m unreliable. But it kind of all circles back to my face and how I’m going to look when I’m there.\n\nI’m trying to think if there’s some other big thing I’m anxious about, but… no, not in the same way. It’s mostly the face thing. That’s the thing that gets stuck in my head and takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't related to how you look?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the same way, at least. I’m trying to think, because I don’t want to just say no if there’s something I’m missing, but nothing really comes to mind that feels like a separate thing.\n\nLike, I don’t have some other urge that I have to act on, or some other thought that keeps looping by itself. Most of the time if I’m stuck on something, it somehow connects back to my face or how I’m going to be seen. If I’m worrying about school or people, it’s usually because I’m already thinking about what I look like when I’m there.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess no. I haven’t really noticed recurring thoughts or urges that aren’t about that.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face changed over time, or have they always been about the left side looking uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly stayed on the left side, yeah. I mean, the exact thing I’m staring at can shift a little—sometimes it’s my nose, sometimes it’s my jaw, sometimes it’s how both of them look together in a photo—but it’s all basically the same problem in my head, like the left side of my face looks off or uneven.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I can’t stop thinking about it. In ID photos it feels really obvious, like my nose is pulled weird or my jaw doesn’t line up right. And on online class camera, it’s even worse because I’ll see myself in that little box and then I can’t pay attention to anything else. I’m just watching my face and thinking, “That looks crooked, everyone can see that.”\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, I know people are probably supposed to be focused on the machines or the work, but I feel like they notice my face first, before they notice anything I’m doing. Even when someone tells me they don’t see it, or that it looks normal, it doesn’t really stick. I still feel like it’s there and everyone else is just being polite or not saying it. So… yeah, it’s pretty much always been that left-side uneven thing, just different versions of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks uneven, or are you more afraid that it might look uneven to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like it actually looks uneven. That’s the hard part. It doesn’t feel like just a “what if” fear to me, like maybe someone might notice something that isn’t there. When I look at myself, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online class, it looks real. Like the left side of my face just looks off, like my nose is crooked or my jaw doesn’t line up the way it’s supposed to.\n\nAnd then once I see it, I can’t unsee it. I’ll try to tell myself maybe it’s the angle or the lighting or the camera, but it still looks wrong to me. And even when other people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it. I feel like they’re being nice, or they just don’t want to say it out loud.\n\nSo I guess it’s both, but mostly I think it really is uneven. And then I’m afraid everyone else can see it too—like in the CNC lab, before they notice my work or anything I’m doing, I feel like they’re noticing that side of my face first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they noticed the unevenness you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I think that’s a big part of why I get so stuck on it. It’s not just that I see the left side of my face as uneven, like my nose and jaw look crooked in photos or on camera, it’s that I immediately imagine other people seeing it too and kind of judging me for it.\n\nLike in ID photos, I feel like it’s frozen there and anyone who looks at it would think, “Why does his face look like that?” And with online class, if my camera is on, I’m not really thinking about the class as much as I’m watching that little box and worrying that everyone else is seeing the same thing I’m seeing. Even if they’re probably not staring at me the whole time, it feels like they could notice it at any second.\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, that’s probably the place where it bothers me the most, because I’m supposed to be focused on the work and the machines and doing things right. But I get this feeling like before anyone notices whether I’m doing a good job, they notice my face first. Like the unevenness is the first thing about me. Even when people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe them. I feel like maybe they’re just being polite, or they noticed but don’t want to make it awkward.\n\nSo, yeah, I do think people would treat me differently, or at least see me differently, if they noticed it. I guess I worry they’d think I look weird, or that it would make them take me less seriously somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop checking and fixing your face if you were completely alone with no chance of being seen by anyone?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I don’t think I would stop completely, no. I wish I could say it’s only because I’m worried other people will see me, but even when I’m by myself, I still get stuck checking. Like before I leave for school, I’ll be in the bathroom mirror looking at the left side of my face, then I’ll switch to my phone camera because somehow I think it’ll show me the “real” version, and then I go back to the mirror again because the phone makes it worse.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole thing where I’m fixing my hair, trying to get it to sit in a way that hides the angle better, or I’ll change shirts because one neckline or color makes my jaw look more obvious to me. It sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like if I can just adjust one thing, maybe it won’t look as uneven.\n\nSometimes I ask my mom, like, “Does this look weird?” or “Does my face look off today?” And even if she says no, I don’t really relax. I might calm down for a minute, but then I check again.\n\nSo if I were completely alone forever, maybe the panic about other people judging me would be less, but I think I’d still be checking and trying to fix it because I’d still see it. It gets so bad some mornings that I spend way too long doing it and I either miss lab or get there feeling drained already, like I used up all my energy before the day even started.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to fix your face if you were certain that no one else could see you or would judge you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would still feel like I had to fix it, even if nobody else could see me. Maybe it wouldn’t have that same panic of, like, “Everyone’s going to notice,” but the urge would still be there because I’d still be seeing it.\n\nLike before I leave for school, I get trapped in this routine where I check my face in the bathroom mirror, especially the left side, and then I use my phone camera because I think maybe that shows it more honestly, but then the phone usually makes it look worse, so I go back to the mirror. And then I’m adjusting my hair, trying to make it fall a certain way so it hides the angle, or I’ll change shirts because one shirt makes my jaw or the unevenness stand out more to me. It’s not always even logical, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask my mom, “Does this look weird?” or “Can you tell my face looks off?” even though I kind of already know I’m not going to fully believe her answer. It’s like I’m trying to get reassurance, but it doesn’t really stick.\n\nSo even if no one could judge me, I think I’d still keep trying to make it look right to myself. Some mornings it takes so long that I miss lab, or I show up already exhausted and can’t focus properly because I’ve spent all that time checking and fixing before I even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if you were certain that it looked perfectly normal to everyone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because if I knew for sure everyone else saw me as normal, maybe I’d be less scared of being judged, but I don’t think the anxiety would just disappear.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off. Like my nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in ID photos or when I see myself on the online class camera. Even if someone told me, “No, it looks perfectly normal,” I’d probably still be sitting there thinking, “But I can see it.” It’s like once I notice it, I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, I know logically people are probably focused on the machines or the work, but I still get this feeling like my face is the first thing that stands out before anything I’m doing. So even if I somehow knew they weren’t noticing it, I think I’d still feel anxious because it bothers me that I notice it. It doesn’t feel normal to me, even if everyone else says it is.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about your face decrease if you were certain that everyone around you perceived it as perfectly normal, but you still saw the unevenness yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it would decrease some, I think. If I could somehow know for sure that everyone around me saw my face as perfectly normal, that would take away part of it — the part where I’m sitting there in the CNC lab thinking people are noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing or any of my work.\n\nBut I don’t think it would go away, because I’d still see it. That’s kind of the problem. I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked. It shows up to me in ID photos, and on the online class camera it feels really obvious, like the angle is just wrong. So even if everybody else said, “No, you look normal,” I’d still be stuck on, “Okay, but I can see the unevenness.”\n\nSo… less anxious maybe, yes, because I wouldn’t feel as exposed to other people. But still anxious, because it would still look wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about your face be the same if you were the only person who could see yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be exactly the same, no… because part of the anxiety is definitely about other people seeing it. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I get this feeling that before anyone notices my work or what I’m doing, they’re noticing that the left side of my face looks off. That my nose or jaw looks crooked. So if I was truly the only person who could see me, maybe that specific fear would be less.\n\nBut I don’t think I’d be calm either. I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it myself. It’s not only, “What if they see it?” It’s also, “I see it.” In every ID photo, or when I’m on the online class camera, it looks wrong to me, like the unevenness is right there. And even when people say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really cancel out what I’m seeing.\n\nSo… maybe it wouldn’t be the same level in terms of embarrassment or feeling watched, but the anxiety would still be there because I’d still be stuck looking at my own face and thinking it’s crooked.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about the fact that you see the unevenness yourself, or about the possibility that others might see it too?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being really honest, it starts with me seeing it myself. Like, I’m the one who gets stuck on the left side of my face looking off — my nose, my jaw, the way it looks crooked in ID photos or on the online class camera. Once I notice it, it’s like my brain keeps going back to it over and over.\n\nBut then it turns into worrying that other people can see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on the work, and I get this feeling that people are noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. Even if someone tells me they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious somehow, like they’re just being polite or not looking closely enough.\n\nSo… I guess the unevenness bothering me when I see it is the main thing, but the fear that everyone else might notice it makes it way worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about what you see when you look at yourself or more about what you think others see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about what I see first, I think… but it doesn’t stay just there. Like, I came in because I honestly can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s like once I see it, I can’t unsee it, and then I keep checking it in my head even when I’m supposed to be focused on something else.\n\nBut then the second part kicks in, where I start thinking other people must see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be working and instead of thinking about the part or the program, I’m thinking, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really reassure me much, because to me it still looks obvious.\n\nSo if I had to choose, it starts with what I see when I look at myself. But what I imagine other people are seeing makes it feel a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face driven more by the fact that you perceive it as flawed, or by the fear that others will evaluate you negatively because of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s driven more by the fact that I see it as flawed, if I’m being honest. Like, before anyone else is even involved, I’m already stuck on it. I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off — like my nose and jaw are crooked — and it shows up that way to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s not just a passing thought. It’s like once I notice it, my mind keeps circling back to it.\n\nBut then the fear of other people judging me kind of builds on top of that. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on my work, and instead I’m thinking that everyone is noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. And even when people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it, or it doesn’t make the feeling go away. I still feel like it must be obvious and they’re just not saying anything.\n\nSo I guess the flaw itself — or what I perceive as the flaw — is the main thing, but the thought that other people are evaluating me because of it makes it feel a lot worse.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s more that I personally don’t like how it looks, I think. Like, it starts with me seeing my face and feeling like the left side is just wrong somehow. My nose and my jaw look crooked to me, and it’s especially bad in ID photos or when I’m on the camera for online class. I’ll look at it and then I can’t stop thinking about it, like it gets stuck in my head.

But then I start thinking other people must see it too. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I feel like people are noticing my face before they notice my work, and that makes it worse. Even if someone says they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really settle me down, because I still feel like it’s obvious.

So… I guess the main thing is that I don’t like how it looks to me, but the idea that other people might not like it, or might be looking at it, makes it feel a lot bigger.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about how my face looks. Specifically the left side. It looks wrong to me, like my nose and jaw are crooked or uneven, and once I notice it I can’t really unsee it.\n\nIt’s especially bad in ID photos or when I see myself on the camera for online classes. I’ll look at it and feel like it’s obvious, like that’s the first thing anyone would notice. And even when people tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, I don’t really believe them. I keep thinking everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing or the work I’m trying to get done. It’s just kind of taking over my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking your face in the mirror, because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. A lot, actually. It’s not just like a quick glance in the mirror. Before I leave for school, I can get completely stuck in the bathroom checking the left side of my face, like turning my head different ways, looking at my nose and jaw, then using my phone camera because I don’t trust the mirror, and then not trusting the phone camera either.\n\nI’ll fix my hair over and over, or change shirts because I think one makes the angle of my face look worse, like if the collar sits a certain way it draws attention to it. And I’ll ask my mom, “Does it look weird? Is it obvious?” Sometimes more than once, even though I know she gets frustrated or says it looks fine. I just don’t believe it for very long.\n\nIt can take so much time that I end up being late or I miss lab altogether. And even if I do make it in, I’m already exhausted from doing all that, so it’s hard to focus on the CNC work. It feels stupid saying it out loud, but I can lose a whole morning to just trying to make my face look acceptable enough to leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these checking or fixing behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like… a magic number exactly. It’s not like I have to check it seven times or something. It’s more like I have to keep doing it until it feels safe enough to leave, and that point keeps moving.\n\nLike I’ll look in the bathroom mirror at the left side of my face, turn my head a little, then I think, no, that angle is lying, so I use my phone camera. Then the phone camera looks worse, so I go back to the mirror, or I try different lighting. I’ll fix my hair because I think maybe if it falls a certain way it hides the uneven part, but then I mess with it too much and it looks stupid, so I start over. Sometimes I change shirts because the collar or neckline makes my jaw look more crooked, or it makes me feel like people will look right there.\n\nAnd I’ll ask my mom, like, “Does it look weird?” or “Can you tell?” but even if she says no, I’ll want to ask again because it only helps for maybe a minute. So it’s not a certain number, but it becomes this whole routine before school, and I can get stuck in it for so long that I miss lab, or if I do go, I’m already drained and can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I worry about school and getting behind, but that’s usually because of this. Like, if I miss lab or show up late, then I start worrying about what that means, or whether people think I’m unreliable. But it kind of all circles back to my face and how I’m going to look when I’m there.\n\nI’m trying to think if there’s some other big thing I’m anxious about, but… no, not in the same way. It’s mostly the face thing. That’s the thing that gets stuck in my head and takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't related to how you look?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the same way, at least. I’m trying to think, because I don’t want to just say no if there’s something I’m missing, but nothing really comes to mind that feels like a separate thing.\n\nLike, I don’t have some other urge that I have to act on, or some other thought that keeps looping by itself. Most of the time if I’m stuck on something, it somehow connects back to my face or how I’m going to be seen. If I’m worrying about school or people, it’s usually because I’m already thinking about what I look like when I’m there.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess no. I haven’t really noticed recurring thoughts or urges that aren’t about that.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face changed over time, or have they always been about the left side looking uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly stayed on the left side, yeah. I mean, the exact thing I’m staring at can shift a little—sometimes it’s my nose, sometimes it’s my jaw, sometimes it’s how both of them look together in a photo—but it’s all basically the same problem in my head, like the left side of my face looks off or uneven.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I can’t stop thinking about it. In ID photos it feels really obvious, like my nose is pulled weird or my jaw doesn’t line up right. And on online class camera, it’s even worse because I’ll see myself in that little box and then I can’t pay attention to anything else. I’m just watching my face and thinking, “That looks crooked, everyone can see that.”\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, I know people are probably supposed to be focused on the machines or the work, but I feel like they notice my face first, before they notice anything I’m doing. Even when someone tells me they don’t see it, or that it looks normal, it doesn’t really stick. I still feel like it’s there and everyone else is just being polite or not saying it. So… yeah, it’s pretty much always been that left-side uneven thing, just different versions of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks uneven, or are you more afraid that it might look uneven to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like it actually looks uneven. That’s the hard part. It doesn’t feel like just a “what if” fear to me, like maybe someone might notice something that isn’t there. When I look at myself, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online class, it looks real. Like the left side of my face just looks off, like my nose is crooked or my jaw doesn’t line up the way it’s supposed to.\n\nAnd then once I see it, I can’t unsee it. I’ll try to tell myself maybe it’s the angle or the lighting or the camera, but it still looks wrong to me. And even when other people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it. I feel like they’re being nice, or they just don’t want to say it out loud.\n\nSo I guess it’s both, but mostly I think it really is uneven. And then I’m afraid everyone else can see it too—like in the CNC lab, before they notice my work or anything I’m doing, I feel like they’re noticing that side of my face first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they noticed the unevenness you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I think that’s a big part of why I get so stuck on it. It’s not just that I see the left side of my face as uneven, like my nose and jaw look crooked in photos or on camera, it’s that I immediately imagine other people seeing it too and kind of judging me for it.\n\nLike in ID photos, I feel like it’s frozen there and anyone who looks at it would think, “Why does his face look like that?” And with online class, if my camera is on, I’m not really thinking about the class as much as I’m watching that little box and worrying that everyone else is seeing the same thing I’m seeing. Even if they’re probably not staring at me the whole time, it feels like they could notice it at any second.\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, that’s probably the place where it bothers me the most, because I’m supposed to be focused on the work and the machines and doing things right. But I get this feeling like before anyone notices whether I’m doing a good job, they notice my face first. Like the unevenness is the first thing about me. Even when people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe them. I feel like maybe they’re just being polite, or they noticed but don’t want to make it awkward.\n\nSo, yeah, I do think people would treat me differently, or at least see me differently, if they noticed it. I guess I worry they’d think I look weird, or that it would make them take me less seriously somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop checking and fixing your face if you were completely alone with no chance of being seen by anyone?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I don’t think I would stop completely, no. I wish I could say it’s only because I’m worried other people will see me, but even when I’m by myself, I still get stuck checking. Like before I leave for school, I’ll be in the bathroom mirror looking at the left side of my face, then I’ll switch to my phone camera because somehow I think it’ll show me the “real” version, and then I go back to the mirror again because the phone makes it worse.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole thing where I’m fixing my hair, trying to get it to sit in a way that hides the angle better, or I’ll change shirts because one neckline or color makes my jaw look more obvious to me. It sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like if I can just adjust one thing, maybe it won’t look as uneven.\n\nSometimes I ask my mom, like, “Does this look weird?” or “Does my face look off today?” And even if she says no, I don’t really relax. I might calm down for a minute, but then I check again.\n\nSo if I were completely alone forever, maybe the panic about other people judging me would be less, but I think I’d still be checking and trying to fix it because I’d still see it. It gets so bad some mornings that I spend way too long doing it and I either miss lab or get there feeling drained already, like I used up all my energy before the day even started.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to fix your face if you were certain that no one else could see you or would judge you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would still feel like I had to fix it, even if nobody else could see me. Maybe it wouldn’t have that same panic of, like, “Everyone’s going to notice,” but the urge would still be there because I’d still be seeing it.\n\nLike before I leave for school, I get trapped in this routine where I check my face in the bathroom mirror, especially the left side, and then I use my phone camera because I think maybe that shows it more honestly, but then the phone usually makes it look worse, so I go back to the mirror. And then I’m adjusting my hair, trying to make it fall a certain way so it hides the angle, or I’ll change shirts because one shirt makes my jaw or the unevenness stand out more to me. It’s not always even logical, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask my mom, “Does this look weird?” or “Can you tell my face looks off?” even though I kind of already know I’m not going to fully believe her answer. It’s like I’m trying to get reassurance, but it doesn’t really stick.\n\nSo even if no one could judge me, I think I’d still keep trying to make it look right to myself. Some mornings it takes so long that I miss lab, or I show up already exhausted and can’t focus properly because I’ve spent all that time checking and fixing before I even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if you were certain that it looked perfectly normal to everyone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because if I knew for sure everyone else saw me as normal, maybe I’d be less scared of being judged, but I don’t think the anxiety would just disappear.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off. Like my nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in ID photos or when I see myself on the online class camera. Even if someone told me, “No, it looks perfectly normal,” I’d probably still be sitting there thinking, “But I can see it.” It’s like once I notice it, I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, I know logically people are probably focused on the machines or the work, but I still get this feeling like my face is the first thing that stands out before anything I’m doing. So even if I somehow knew they weren’t noticing it, I think I’d still feel anxious because it bothers me that I notice it. It doesn’t feel normal to me, even if everyone else says it is.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about your face decrease if you were certain that everyone around you perceived it as perfectly normal, but you still saw the unevenness yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it would decrease some, I think. If I could somehow know for sure that everyone around me saw my face as perfectly normal, that would take away part of it — the part where I’m sitting there in the CNC lab thinking people are noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing or any of my work.\n\nBut I don’t think it would go away, because I’d still see it. That’s kind of the problem. I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked. It shows up to me in ID photos, and on the online class camera it feels really obvious, like the angle is just wrong. So even if everybody else said, “No, you look normal,” I’d still be stuck on, “Okay, but I can see the unevenness.”\n\nSo… less anxious maybe, yes, because I wouldn’t feel as exposed to other people. But still anxious, because it would still look wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about your face be the same if you were the only person who could see yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be exactly the same, no… because part of the anxiety is definitely about other people seeing it. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I get this feeling that before anyone notices my work or what I’m doing, they’re noticing that the left side of my face looks off. That my nose or jaw looks crooked. So if I was truly the only person who could see me, maybe that specific fear would be less.\n\nBut I don’t think I’d be calm either. I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it myself. It’s not only, “What if they see it?” It’s also, “I see it.” In every ID photo, or when I’m on the online class camera, it looks wrong to me, like the unevenness is right there. And even when people say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really cancel out what I’m seeing.\n\nSo… maybe it wouldn’t be the same level in terms of embarrassment or feeling watched, but the anxiety would still be there because I’d still be stuck looking at my own face and thinking it’s crooked.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about the fact that you see the unevenness yourself, or about the possibility that others might see it too?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being really honest, it starts with me seeing it myself. Like, I’m the one who gets stuck on the left side of my face looking off — my nose, my jaw, the way it looks crooked in ID photos or on the online class camera. Once I notice it, it’s like my brain keeps going back to it over and over.\n\nBut then it turns into worrying that other people can see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on the work, and I get this feeling that people are noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. Even if someone tells me they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious somehow, like they’re just being polite or not looking closely enough.\n\nSo… I guess the unevenness bothering me when I see it is the main thing, but the fear that everyone else might notice it makes it way worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about what you see when you look at yourself or more about what you think others see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about what I see first, I think… but it doesn’t stay just there. Like, I came in because I honestly can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s like once I see it, I can’t unsee it, and then I keep checking it in my head even when I’m supposed to be focused on something else.\n\nBut then the second part kicks in, where I start thinking other people must see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be working and instead of thinking about the part or the program, I’m thinking, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really reassure me much, because to me it still looks obvious.\n\nSo if I had to choose, it starts with what I see when I look at myself. But what I imagine other people are seeing makes it feel a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face driven more by the fact that you perceive it as flawed, or by the fear that others will evaluate you negatively because of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s driven more by the fact that I see it as flawed, if I’m being honest. Like, before anyone else is even involved, I’m already stuck on it. I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off — like my nose and jaw are crooked — and it shows up that way to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s not just a passing thought. It’s like once I notice it, my mind keeps circling back to it.\n\nBut then the fear of other people judging me kind of builds on top of that. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on my work, and instead I’m thinking that everyone is noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. And even when people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it, or it doesn’t make the feeling go away. I still feel like it must be obvious and they’re just not saying anything.\n\nSo I guess the flaw itself — or what I perceive as the flaw — is the main thing, but the thought that other people are evaluating me because of it makes it feel a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Is it more the fact that you personally don't like how your face looks that bothers you, or is it more the idea that others might not like how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I personally don’t like how it looks, I think. Like, it starts with me seeing my face and feeling like the left side is just wrong somehow. My nose and my jaw look crooked to me, and it’s especially bad in ID photos or when I’m on the camera for online class. I’ll look at it and then I can’t stop thinking about it, like it gets stuck in my head.\n\nBut then I start thinking other people must see it too. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I feel like people are noticing my face before they notice my work, and that makes it worse. Even if someone says they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really settle me down, because I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nSo… I guess the main thing is that I don’t like how it looks to me, but the idea that other people might not like it, or might be looking at it, makes it feel a lot bigger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========

I wish I could say it’s only because I’m worried other people will see me, but even when I’m by myself, I still get stuck checking. Like before I leave for school, I’ll be in the bathroom mirror looking at the left side of my face, then I’ll switch to my phone camera because somehow I think it’ll show me the “real” version, and then I go back to the mirror again because the phone makes it worse.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole thing where I’m fixing my hair, trying to get it to sit in a way that hides the angle better, or I’ll change shirts because one neckline or color makes my jaw look more obvious to me. It sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like if I can just adjust one thing, maybe it won’t look as uneven.\n\nSometimes I ask my mom, like, “Does this look weird?” or “Does my face look off today?” And even if she says no, I don’t really relax. I might calm down for a minute, but then I check again.\n\nSo if I were completely alone forever, maybe the panic about other people judging me would be less, but I think I’d still be checking and trying to fix it because I’d still see it. It gets so bad some mornings that I spend way too long doing it and I either miss lab or get there feeling drained already, like I used up all my energy before the day even started.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to fix your face if you were certain that no one else could see you or would judge you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would still feel like I had to fix it, even if nobody else could see me. Maybe it wouldn’t have that same panic of, like, “Everyone’s going to notice,” but the urge would still be there because I’d still be seeing it.\n\nLike before I leave for school, I get trapped in this routine where I check my face in the bathroom mirror, especially the left side, and then I use my phone camera because I think maybe that shows it more honestly, but then the phone usually makes it look worse, so I go back to the mirror. And then I’m adjusting my hair, trying to make it fall a certain way so it hides the angle, or I’ll change shirts because one shirt makes my jaw or the unevenness stand out more to me. It’s not always even logical, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask my mom, “Does this look weird?” or “Can you tell my face looks off?” even though I kind of already know I’m not going to fully believe her answer. It’s like I’m trying to get reassurance, but it doesn’t really stick.\n\nSo even if no one could judge me, I think I’d still keep trying to make it look right to myself. Some mornings it takes so long that I miss lab, or I show up already exhausted and can’t focus properly because I’ve spent all that time checking and fixing before I even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if you were certain that it looked perfectly normal to everyone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because if I knew for sure everyone else saw me as normal, maybe I’d be less scared of being judged, but I don’t think the anxiety would just disappear.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off. Like my nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in ID photos or when I see myself on the online class camera. Even if someone told me, “No, it looks perfectly normal,” I’d probably still be sitting there thinking, “But I can see it.” It’s like once I notice it, I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, I know logically people are probably focused on the machines or the work, but I still get this feeling like my face is the first thing that stands out before anything I’m doing. So even if I somehow knew they weren’t noticing it, I think I’d still feel anxious because it bothers me that I notice it. It doesn’t feel normal to me, even if everyone else says it is.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about your face decrease if you were certain that everyone around you perceived it as perfectly normal, but you still saw the unevenness yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it would decrease some, I think. If I could somehow know for sure that everyone around me saw my face as perfectly normal, that would take away part of it — the part where I’m sitting there in the CNC lab thinking people are noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing or any of my work.\n\nBut I don’t think it would go away, because I’d still see it. That’s kind of the problem. I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked. It shows up to me in ID photos, and on the online class camera it feels really obvious, like the angle is just wrong. So even if everybody else said, “No, you look normal,” I’d still be stuck on, “Okay, but I can see the unevenness.”\n\nSo… less anxious maybe, yes, because I wouldn’t feel as exposed to other people. But still anxious, because it would still look wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about your face be the same if you were the only person who could see yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be exactly the same, no… because part of the anxiety is definitely about other people seeing it. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I get this feeling that before anyone notices my work or what I’m doing, they’re noticing that the left side of my face looks off. That my nose or jaw looks crooked. So if I was truly the only person who could see me, maybe that specific fear would be less.\n\nBut I don’t think I’d be calm either. I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it myself. It’s not only, “What if they see it?” It’s also, “I see it.” In every ID photo, or when I’m on the online class camera, it looks wrong to me, like the unevenness is right there. And even when people say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really cancel out what I’m seeing.\n\nSo… maybe it wouldn’t be the same level in terms of embarrassment or feeling watched, but the anxiety would still be there because I’d still be stuck looking at my own face and thinking it’s crooked.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about the fact that you see the unevenness yourself, or about the possibility that others might see it too?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being really honest, it starts with me seeing it myself. Like, I’m the one who gets stuck on the left side of my face looking off — my nose, my jaw, the way it looks crooked in ID photos or on the online class camera. Once I notice it, it’s like my brain keeps going back to it over and over.\n\nBut then it turns into worrying that other people can see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on the work, and I get this feeling that people are noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. Even if someone tells me they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious somehow, like they’re just being polite or not looking closely enough.\n\nSo… I guess the unevenness bothering me when I see it is the main thing, but the fear that everyone else might notice it makes it way worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about what you see when you look at yourself or more about what you think others see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about what I see first, I think… but it doesn’t stay just there. Like, I came in because I honestly can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s like once I see it, I can’t unsee it, and then I keep checking it in my head even when I’m supposed to be focused on something else.\n\nBut then the second part kicks in, where I start thinking other people must see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be working and instead of thinking about the part or the program, I’m thinking, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really reassure me much, because to me it still looks obvious.\n\nSo if I had to choose, it starts with what I see when I look at myself. But what I imagine other people are seeing makes it feel a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face driven more by the fact that you perceive it as flawed, or by the fear that others will evaluate you negatively because of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s driven more by the fact that I see it as flawed, if I’m being honest. Like, before anyone else is even involved, I’m already stuck on it. I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off — like my nose and jaw are crooked — and it shows up that way to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s not just a passing thought. It’s like once I notice it, my mind keeps circling back to it.\n\nBut then the fear of other people judging me kind of builds on top of that. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on my work, and instead I’m thinking that everyone is noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. And even when people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it, or it doesn’t make the feeling go away. I still feel like it must be obvious and they’re just not saying anything.\n\nSo I guess the flaw itself — or what I perceive as the flaw — is the main thing, but the thought that other people are evaluating me because of it makes it feel a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Is it more the fact that you personally don't like how your face looks that bothers you, or is it more the idea that others might not like how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I personally don’t like how it looks, I think. Like, it starts with me seeing my face and feeling like the left side is just wrong somehow. My nose and my jaw look crooked to me, and it’s especially bad in ID photos or when I’m on the camera for online class. I’ll look at it and then I can’t stop thinking about it, like it gets stuck in my head.\n\nBut then I start thinking other people must see it too. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I feel like people are noticing my face before they notice my work, and that makes it worse. Even if someone says they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really settle me down, because I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nSo… I guess the main thing is that I don’t like how it looks to me, but the idea that other people might not like it, or might be looking at it, makes it feel a lot bigger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I think it would still be there. Maybe it wouldn’t be as intense in the exact same way, because the part about people judging me or noticing it in the CNC lab makes it spike, but the original problem is still what I see.

Like even if I knew no one else could see me, I’d still look at an ID photo or see myself on the online class camera and immediately feel like the left side of my face looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me, and once I notice it, it’s like my brain keeps coming back to it over and over. It doesn’t really depend on someone else pointing it out.

So if no one could judge me, I think I’d still be anxious and stuck on it. The fear that people notice it makes it worse, but it’s not the only reason it bothers me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about how my face looks. Specifically the left side. It looks wrong to me, like my nose and jaw are crooked or uneven, and once I notice it I can’t really unsee it.\n\nIt’s especially bad in ID photos or when I see myself on the camera for online classes. I’ll look at it and feel like it’s obvious, like that’s the first thing anyone would notice. And even when people tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, I don’t really believe them. I keep thinking everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing or the work I’m trying to get done. It’s just kind of taking over my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking your face in the mirror, because of these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. A lot, actually. It’s not just like a quick glance in the mirror. Before I leave for school, I can get completely stuck in the bathroom checking the left side of my face, like turning my head different ways, looking at my nose and jaw, then using my phone camera because I don’t trust the mirror, and then not trusting the phone camera either.\n\nI’ll fix my hair over and over, or change shirts because I think one makes the angle of my face look worse, like if the collar sits a certain way it draws attention to it. And I’ll ask my mom, “Does it look weird? Is it obvious?” Sometimes more than once, even though I know she gets frustrated or says it looks fine. I just don’t believe it for very long.\n\nIt can take so much time that I end up being late or I miss lab altogether. And even if I do make it in, I’m already exhausted from doing all that, so it’s hard to focus on the CNC work. It feels stupid saying it out loud, but I can lose a whole morning to just trying to make my face look acceptable enough to leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these checking or fixing behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like… a magic number exactly. It’s not like I have to check it seven times or something. It’s more like I have to keep doing it until it feels safe enough to leave, and that point keeps moving.\n\nLike I’ll look in the bathroom mirror at the left side of my face, turn my head a little, then I think, no, that angle is lying, so I use my phone camera. Then the phone camera looks worse, so I go back to the mirror, or I try different lighting. I’ll fix my hair because I think maybe if it falls a certain way it hides the uneven part, but then I mess with it too much and it looks stupid, so I start over. Sometimes I change shirts because the collar or neckline makes my jaw look more crooked, or it makes me feel like people will look right there.\n\nAnd I’ll ask my mom, like, “Does it look weird?” or “Can you tell?” but even if she says no, I’ll want to ask again because it only helps for maybe a minute. So it’s not a certain number, but it becomes this whole routine before school, and I can get stuck in it for so long that I miss lab, or if I do go, I’m already drained and can’t really focus.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I worry about school and getting behind, but that’s usually because of this. Like, if I miss lab or show up late, then I start worrying about what that means, or whether people think I’m unreliable. But it kind of all circles back to my face and how I’m going to look when I’m there.\n\nI’m trying to think if there’s some other big thing I’m anxious about, but… no, not in the same way. It’s mostly the face thing. That’s the thing that gets stuck in my head and takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that aren't related to how you look?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the same way, at least. I’m trying to think, because I don’t want to just say no if there’s something I’m missing, but nothing really comes to mind that feels like a separate thing.\n\nLike, I don’t have some other urge that I have to act on, or some other thought that keeps looping by itself. Most of the time if I’m stuck on something, it somehow connects back to my face or how I’m going to be seen. If I’m worrying about school or people, it’s usually because I’m already thinking about what I look like when I’m there.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess no. I haven’t really noticed recurring thoughts or urges that aren’t about that.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face changed over time, or have they always been about the left side looking uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly stayed on the left side, yeah. I mean, the exact thing I’m staring at can shift a little—sometimes it’s my nose, sometimes it’s my jaw, sometimes it’s how both of them look together in a photo—but it’s all basically the same problem in my head, like the left side of my face looks off or uneven.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I can’t stop thinking about it. In ID photos it feels really obvious, like my nose is pulled weird or my jaw doesn’t line up right. And on online class camera, it’s even worse because I’ll see myself in that little box and then I can’t pay attention to anything else. I’m just watching my face and thinking, “That looks crooked, everyone can see that.”\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, I know people are probably supposed to be focused on the machines or the work, but I feel like they notice my face first, before they notice anything I’m doing. Even when someone tells me they don’t see it, or that it looks normal, it doesn’t really stick. I still feel like it’s there and everyone else is just being polite or not saying it. So… yeah, it’s pretty much always been that left-side uneven thing, just different versions of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks uneven, or are you more afraid that it might look uneven to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like it actually looks uneven. That’s the hard part. It doesn’t feel like just a “what if” fear to me, like maybe someone might notice something that isn’t there. When I look at myself, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online class, it looks real. Like the left side of my face just looks off, like my nose is crooked or my jaw doesn’t line up the way it’s supposed to.\n\nAnd then once I see it, I can’t unsee it. I’ll try to tell myself maybe it’s the angle or the lighting or the camera, but it still looks wrong to me. And even when other people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it. I feel like they’re being nice, or they just don’t want to say it out loud.\n\nSo I guess it’s both, but mostly I think it really is uneven. And then I’m afraid everyone else can see it too—like in the CNC lab, before they notice my work or anything I’m doing, I feel like they’re noticing that side of my face first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they noticed the unevenness you see in your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I think that’s a big part of why I get so stuck on it. It’s not just that I see the left side of my face as uneven, like my nose and jaw look crooked in photos or on camera, it’s that I immediately imagine other people seeing it too and kind of judging me for it.\n\nLike in ID photos, I feel like it’s frozen there and anyone who looks at it would think, “Why does his face look like that?” And with online class, if my camera is on, I’m not really thinking about the class as much as I’m watching that little box and worrying that everyone else is seeing the same thing I’m seeing. Even if they’re probably not staring at me the whole time, it feels like they could notice it at any second.\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, that’s probably the place where it bothers me the most, because I’m supposed to be focused on the work and the machines and doing things right. But I get this feeling like before anyone notices whether I’m doing a good job, they notice my face first. Like the unevenness is the first thing about me. Even when people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe them. I feel like maybe they’re just being polite, or they noticed but don’t want to make it awkward.\n\nSo, yeah, I do think people would treat me differently, or at least see me differently, if they noticed it. I guess I worry they’d think I look weird, or that it would make them take me less seriously somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop checking and fixing your face if you were completely alone with no chance of being seen by anyone?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I don’t think I would stop completely, no. I wish I could say it’s only because I’m worried other people will see me, but even when I’m by myself, I still get stuck checking. Like before I leave for school, I’ll be in the bathroom mirror looking at the left side of my face, then I’ll switch to my phone camera because somehow I think it’ll show me the “real” version, and then I go back to the mirror again because the phone makes it worse.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole thing where I’m fixing my hair, trying to get it to sit in a way that hides the angle better, or I’ll change shirts because one neckline or color makes my jaw look more obvious to me. It sounds stupid when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like if I can just adjust one thing, maybe it won’t look as uneven.\n\nSometimes I ask my mom, like, “Does this look weird?” or “Does my face look off today?” And even if she says no, I don’t really relax. I might calm down for a minute, but then I check again.\n\nSo if I were completely alone forever, maybe the panic about other people judging me would be less, but I think I’d still be checking and trying to fix it because I’d still see it. It gets so bad some mornings that I spend way too long doing it and I either miss lab or get there feeling drained already, like I used up all my energy before the day even started.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to fix your face if you were certain that no one else could see you or would judge you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would still feel like I had to fix it, even if nobody else could see me. Maybe it wouldn’t have that same panic of, like, “Everyone’s going to notice,” but the urge would still be there because I’d still be seeing it.\n\nLike before I leave for school, I get trapped in this routine where I check my face in the bathroom mirror, especially the left side, and then I use my phone camera because I think maybe that shows it more honestly, but then the phone usually makes it look worse, so I go back to the mirror. And then I’m adjusting my hair, trying to make it fall a certain way so it hides the angle, or I’ll change shirts because one shirt makes my jaw or the unevenness stand out more to me. It’s not always even logical, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask my mom, “Does this look weird?” or “Can you tell my face looks off?” even though I kind of already know I’m not going to fully believe her answer. It’s like I’m trying to get reassurance, but it doesn’t really stick.\n\nSo even if no one could judge me, I think I’d still keep trying to make it look right to myself. Some mornings it takes so long that I miss lab, or I show up already exhausted and can’t focus properly because I’ve spent all that time checking and fixing before I even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if you were certain that it looked perfectly normal to everyone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because if I knew for sure everyone else saw me as normal, maybe I’d be less scared of being judged, but I don’t think the anxiety would just disappear.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off. Like my nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in ID photos or when I see myself on the online class camera. Even if someone told me, “No, it looks perfectly normal,” I’d probably still be sitting there thinking, “But I can see it.” It’s like once I notice it, I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd in the CNC lab, I know logically people are probably focused on the machines or the work, but I still get this feeling like my face is the first thing that stands out before anything I’m doing. So even if I somehow knew they weren’t noticing it, I think I’d still feel anxious because it bothers me that I notice it. It doesn’t feel normal to me, even if everyone else says it is.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about your face decrease if you were certain that everyone around you perceived it as perfectly normal, but you still saw the unevenness yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it would decrease some, I think. If I could somehow know for sure that everyone around me saw my face as perfectly normal, that would take away part of it — the part where I’m sitting there in the CNC lab thinking people are noticing my face before they notice anything I’m doing or any of my work.\n\nBut I don’t think it would go away, because I’d still see it. That’s kind of the problem. I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off, like my nose and jaw are crooked. It shows up to me in ID photos, and on the online class camera it feels really obvious, like the angle is just wrong. So even if everybody else said, “No, you look normal,” I’d still be stuck on, “Okay, but I can see the unevenness.”\n\nSo… less anxious maybe, yes, because I wouldn’t feel as exposed to other people. But still anxious, because it would still look wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about your face be the same if you were the only person who could see yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be exactly the same, no… because part of the anxiety is definitely about other people seeing it. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I get this feeling that before anyone notices my work or what I’m doing, they’re noticing that the left side of my face looks off. That my nose or jaw looks crooked. So if I was truly the only person who could see me, maybe that specific fear would be less.\n\nBut I don’t think I’d be calm either. I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it myself. It’s not only, “What if they see it?” It’s also, “I see it.” In every ID photo, or when I’m on the online class camera, it looks wrong to me, like the unevenness is right there. And even when people say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really cancel out what I’m seeing.\n\nSo… maybe it wouldn’t be the same level in terms of embarrassment or feeling watched, but the anxiety would still be there because I’d still be stuck looking at my own face and thinking it’s crooked.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about the fact that you see the unevenness yourself, or about the possibility that others might see it too?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being really honest, it starts with me seeing it myself. Like, I’m the one who gets stuck on the left side of my face looking off — my nose, my jaw, the way it looks crooked in ID photos or on the online class camera. Once I notice it, it’s like my brain keeps going back to it over and over.\n\nBut then it turns into worrying that other people can see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on the work, and I get this feeling that people are noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. Even if someone tells me they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious somehow, like they’re just being polite or not looking closely enough.\n\nSo… I guess the unevenness bothering me when I see it is the main thing, but the fear that everyone else might notice it makes it way worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about what you see when you look at yourself or more about what you think others see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about what I see first, I think… but it doesn’t stay just there. Like, I came in because I honestly can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s like once I see it, I can’t unsee it, and then I keep checking it in my head even when I’m supposed to be focused on something else.\n\nBut then the second part kicks in, where I start thinking other people must see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be working and instead of thinking about the part or the program, I’m thinking, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really reassure me much, because to me it still looks obvious.\n\nSo if I had to choose, it starts with what I see when I look at myself. But what I imagine other people are seeing makes it feel a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face driven more by the fact that you perceive it as flawed, or by the fear that others will evaluate you negatively because of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s driven more by the fact that I see it as flawed, if I’m being honest. Like, before anyone else is even involved, I’m already stuck on it. I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off — like my nose and jaw are crooked — and it shows up that way to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s not just a passing thought. It’s like once I notice it, my mind keeps circling back to it.\n\nBut then the fear of other people judging me kind of builds on top of that. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on my work, and instead I’m thinking that everyone is noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. And even when people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it, or it doesn’t make the feeling go away. I still feel like it must be obvious and they’re just not saying anything.\n\nSo I guess the flaw itself — or what I perceive as the flaw — is the main thing, but the thought that other people are evaluating me because of it makes it feel a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Is it more the fact that you personally don't like how your face looks that bothers you, or is it more the idea that others might not like how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I personally don’t like how it looks, I think. Like, it starts with me seeing my face and feeling like the left side is just wrong somehow. My nose and my jaw look crooked to me, and it’s especially bad in ID photos or when I’m on the camera for online class. I’ll look at it and then I can’t stop thinking about it, like it gets stuck in my head.\n\nBut then I start thinking other people must see it too. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I feel like people are noticing my face before they notice my work, and that makes it worse. Even if someone says they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really settle me down, because I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nSo… I guess the main thing is that I don’t like how it looks to me, but the idea that other people might not like it, or might be looking at it, makes it feel a lot bigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face something that you think would still be present if you knew for certain that no one else could see you or judge your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it would still be there. Maybe it wouldn’t be as intense in the exact same way, because the part about people judging me or noticing it in the CNC lab makes it spike, but the original problem is still what I see.\n\nLike even if I knew no one else could see me, I’d still look at an ID photo or see myself on the online class camera and immediately feel like the left side of my face looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me, and once I notice it, it’s like my brain keeps coming back to it over and over. It doesn’t really depend on someone else pointing it out.\n\nOther people not seeing it doesn’t really fix it either. They’ll say they don’t notice anything, but I still feel like it’s obvious when I look at myself. So if no one could judge me, I think I’d still be anxious and stuck on it. The fear that people notice it makes it worse, but it’s not the only reason it bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So even if everybody else said, “No, you look normal,” I’d still be stuck on, “Okay, but I can see the unevenness.”\n\nSo… less anxious maybe, yes, because I wouldn’t feel as exposed to other people. But still anxious, because it would still look wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about your face be the same if you were the only person who could see yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be exactly the same, no… because part of the anxiety is definitely about other people seeing it. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I get this feeling that before anyone notices my work or what I’m doing, they’re noticing that the left side of my face looks off. That my nose or jaw looks crooked. So if I was truly the only person who could see me, maybe that specific fear would be less.\n\nBut I don’t think I’d be calm either. I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it myself. It’s not only, “What if they see it?” It’s also, “I see it.” In every ID photo, or when I’m on the online class camera, it looks wrong to me, like the unevenness is right there. And even when people say they don’t see it, it doesn’t really cancel out what I’m seeing.\n\nSo… maybe it wouldn’t be the same level in terms of embarrassment or feeling watched, but the anxiety would still be there because I’d still be stuck looking at my own face and thinking it’s crooked.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about the fact that you see the unevenness yourself, or about the possibility that others might see it too?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being really honest, it starts with me seeing it myself. Like, I’m the one who gets stuck on the left side of my face looking off — my nose, my jaw, the way it looks crooked in ID photos or on the online class camera. Once I notice it, it’s like my brain keeps going back to it over and over.\n\nBut then it turns into worrying that other people can see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on the work, and I get this feeling that people are noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. Even if someone tells me they don’t see anything, I still feel like it’s obvious somehow, like they’re just being polite or not looking closely enough.\n\nSo… I guess the unevenness bothering me when I see it is the main thing, but the fear that everyone else might notice it makes it way worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face more about what you see when you look at yourself or more about what you think others see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about what I see first, I think… but it doesn’t stay just there. Like, I came in because I honestly can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s like once I see it, I can’t unsee it, and then I keep checking it in my head even when I’m supposed to be focused on something else.\n\nBut then the second part kicks in, where I start thinking other people must see it too. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be working and instead of thinking about the part or the program, I’m thinking, “They’re noticing my face before they notice my work.” Even when people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really reassure me much, because to me it still looks obvious.\n\nSo if I had to choose, it starts with what I see when I look at myself. But what I imagine other people are seeing makes it feel a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face driven more by the fact that you perceive it as flawed, or by the fear that others will evaluate you negatively because of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s driven more by the fact that I see it as flawed, if I’m being honest. Like, before anyone else is even involved, I’m already stuck on it. I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off — like my nose and jaw are crooked — and it shows up that way to me in every ID photo or when I’m on the online class camera. It’s not just a passing thought. It’s like once I notice it, my mind keeps circling back to it.\n\nBut then the fear of other people judging me kind of builds on top of that. Like in the CNC lab, I’ll be trying to focus on my work, and instead I’m thinking that everyone is noticing my face before they notice what I’m doing. And even when people say they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it, or it doesn’t make the feeling go away. I still feel like it must be obvious and they’re just not saying anything.\n\nSo I guess the flaw itself — or what I perceive as the flaw — is the main thing, but the thought that other people are evaluating me because of it makes it feel a lot worse.\n\nDoctor: Is it more the fact that you personally don't like how your face looks that bothers you, or is it more the idea that others might not like how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I personally don’t like how it looks, I think. Like, it starts with me seeing my face and feeling like the left side is just wrong somehow. My nose and my jaw look crooked to me, and it’s especially bad in ID photos or when I’m on the camera for online class. I’ll look at it and then I can’t stop thinking about it, like it gets stuck in my head.\n\nBut then I start thinking other people must see it too. Like when I’m in the CNC lab, I feel like people are noticing my face before they notice my work, and that makes it worse. Even if someone says they don’t see what I’m talking about, it doesn’t really settle me down, because I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nSo… I guess the main thing is that I don’t like how it looks to me, but the idea that other people might not like it, or might be looking at it, makes it feel a lot bigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about your face something that you think would still be present if you knew for certain that no one else could see you or judge your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it would still be there. Maybe it wouldn’t be as intense in the exact same way, because the part about people judging me or noticing it in the CNC lab makes it spike, but the original problem is still what I see.\n\nLike even if I knew no one else could see me, I’d still look at an ID photo or see myself on the online class camera and immediately feel like the left side of my face looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me, and once I notice it, it’s like my brain keeps coming back to it over and over. It doesn’t really depend on someone else pointing it out.\n\nOther people not seeing it doesn’t really fix it either. They’ll say they don’t notice anything, but I still feel like it’s obvious when I look at myself. So if no one could judge me, I think I’d still be anxious and stuck on it. The fear that people notice it makes it worse, but it’s not the only reason it bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s mainly because I perceive the flaw myself. Like, I know I’ve said this a few times, but that’s really the center of it — I came in because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks off. My nose and jaw look crooked to me, especially in ID photos or when I see myself on the online class camera. It’s like once I see it, I can’t unsee it, and then I keep checking it in my head over and over.

Even if someone tells me they don’t notice anything, I don’t really believe it, or it doesn’t make the feeling go away. I still feel like it has to be obvious.

So I’d say it starts with the flaw I see, and then the fear of other people judging me makes it worse.
